of <a href="/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam" title="Jesus in Islam">Jesus</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" id="cite_ref-M._Limberis_2011_164_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-M._Limberis_2011_164-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Influences</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Influenced</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Ignatius of Antioch">Ignatius of Antioch</a>, <a href="/wiki/Polycarp" title="Polycarp">Polycarp</a>, <a href="/wiki/Papias_of_Hierapolis" title="Papias of Hierapolis">Papias of Hierapolis</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Odes_of_Solomon" title="Odes of Solomon">Odes of Solomon</a></i> <sup id="cite_ref-:0_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>John the Apostle</b><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Greek language">Ancient Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Ἰωάννης</span>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ioannes</i><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> <span class="nowrap"><abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;6 AD</span> – <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;100 AD</span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Ge%CA%BDez" title="Geʽez">Ge'ez</a>: ዮሐንስ;) or <b>Saint John the Beloved</b> was one of the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve Apostles">Twelve Apostles</a> of <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> according to the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>. Generally listed as the youngest apostle, he was the son of <a href="/wiki/Zebedee" title="Zebedee">Zebedee</a> and <a href="/wiki/Salome_(disciple)" title="Salome (disciple)">Salome</a>. His brother <a href="/wiki/James_the_Great" title="James the Great">James</a> was another of the Twelve Apostles. The <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a> identify him as <a href="/wiki/John_the_Evangelist" title="John the Evangelist">John the Evangelist</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_of_Patmos" title="John of Patmos">John of Patmos</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_the_Presbyter" title="John the Presbyter">John the Elder</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Disciple_whom_Jesus_loved" title="Disciple whom Jesus loved">Beloved Disciple</a>, and testify that he outlived the remaining apostles and was the only one to die of natural causes, although modern scholars are divided on the veracity of these claims. 
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena,_Italy.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/John_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/220px-John_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/John_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/330px-John_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/John_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/440px-John_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2435" data-file-height="1468" /></a><figcaption>John the Apostle, detail of the mosaic in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Vitale" title="Basilica of San Vitale">Basilica of San Vitale</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ravenna" title="Ravenna">Ravenna</a>, 6th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>John the Apostle is traditionally held to be the author of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a>, and many Christian denominations believe that he authored several other books of the New Testament (the three <a href="/wiki/Johannine_epistles" title="Johannine epistles">Johannine epistles</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a>, together with the Gospel of John, are called the <a href="/wiki/Johannine_works" class="mw-redirect" title="Johannine works">Johannine works</a>), depending on whether he is distinguished from, or identified with, John the Evangelist, John the Elder, and John of Patmos.
</p><p>Although the <a href="/wiki/Authorship_of_the_Johannine_works" title="Authorship of the Johannine works">authorship of the Johannine works</a> has traditionally been attributed to John the Apostle,<sup id="cite_ref-harris_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-harris-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> only a minority of contemporary scholars believe he wrote the gospel,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELindarsEdwardsCourt200041_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELindarsEdwardsCourt200041-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and most conclude that he wrote none of them.<sup id="cite_ref-harris_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-harris-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Kelly2012_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kelly2012-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Harris_1980_p._15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harris_1980_p.-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Regardless of whether or not John the Apostle wrote any of the Johannine works, most scholars agree that all three epistles were written by the same author and that the epistles did not have the same author as the Book of Revelation, although there is widespread disagreement among scholars as to whether the author of the epistles was different from that of the gospel.<sup id="cite_ref-books.google.com_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-books.google.com-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-The_Gospel_and_Epistles_of_John:_A_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Gospel_and_Epistles_of_John:_A-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-The_Epistles_of_John_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Epistles_of_John-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References_to_John_in_the_New_Testament">References to John in the New Testament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: References to John in the New Testament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Evangelist.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/John_Evangelist.jpg/180px-John_Evangelist.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="390" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c4/John_Evangelist.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="231" data-file-height="500" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Orthodox">Russian Orthodox</a> icon of the Apostle and Evangelist John the Theologian, 18th century (<a href="/wiki/Iconostasis" title="Iconostasis">Iconostasis</a> from the Church of the <a href="/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus" title="Transfiguration of Jesus">Transfiguration</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kizhi" class="mw-redirect" title="Kizhi">Kizhi</a> Monastery</figcaption></figure>
<p>John the Apostle was the son of Zebedee and the younger brother of <a href="/wiki/James_the_Great" title="James the Great">James the Great</a>. According to church tradition, their mother was <a href="/wiki/Salome_(disciple)" title="Salome (disciple)">Salome</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Also according to some traditions, Salome was the sister of <a href="/wiki/Mary,_mother_of_Jesus" title="Mary, mother of Jesus">Mary, Jesus' mother</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-:1_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> making Salome Jesus' aunt, and her sons John the Apostle and James were Jesus' cousins.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John the Apostle is traditionally believed to be one of two disciples (the other being <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew</a>) recounted in <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+1:35–39&amp;version=nkjv">John 1:35–39</a>, who upon hearing the Baptist point out Jesus as the "Lamb of God", followed Jesus and spent the day with him, thus becoming the first two disciples called by Jesus. On this basis some traditions believe that John was first a disciple of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John the Baptist</a>, even though he is not named in this episode.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels" title="Synoptic Gospels">Synoptic Gospels</a> (Matt 4:18–22; Mark 1:16–20; Lk 5:1–11), Zebedee and his sons fished in the <a href="/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee" title="Sea of Galilee">Sea of Galilee</a>. Jesus then called <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Andrew" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Andrew">Andrew</a> and the two sons of Zebedee to follow him. James and John are listed among the Twelve Apostles. Jesus referred to the pair as "Boanerges" (translated "sons of thunder").<sup id="cite_ref-foley_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-foley-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> A Gospel story relates how the brothers wanted to call down heavenly fire on an unhospitable <a href="/wiki/Samaritan" class="mw-redirect" title="Samaritan">Samaritan</a> town, but Jesus rebuked them.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> John was also the disciple who reported to Jesus that they had 'forbidden' a non-disciple from <a href="/wiki/Exorcism" title="Exorcism">casting out demons</a> in Jesus' name, prompting Jesus to state that 'he who is not against us is on our side'.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John lived on for more than fifty years after the martyrdom of his brother James<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="We have no information at all what John did or did not do after pentecost. There are no sources. (September 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Position.2C_who_became_the_first_Apostle_to_die_a_martyr.27s_death_in_AD_44._among_the_apostles"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Position,_who_became_the_first_Apostle_to_die_a_martyr's_death_in_AD_44._among_the_apostles">Position, who became the first Apostle to die a <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyr" title="Christian martyr">martyr's death</a> in AD 44. among the apostles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Position, who became the first Apostle to die a martyr&#039;s death in AD 44. among the apostles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer_-_The_Four_Holy_Men_(John_the_Evangelist_and_Peter)_-_WGA7025.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer_-_The_Four_Holy_Men_%28John_the_Evangelist_and_Peter%29_-_WGA7025.jpg/130px-Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer_-_The_Four_Holy_Men_%28John_the_Evangelist_and_Peter%29_-_WGA7025.jpg" decoding="async" width="130" height="375" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer_-_The_Four_Holy_Men_%28John_the_Evangelist_and_Peter%29_-_WGA7025.jpg/195px-Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer_-_The_Four_Holy_Men_%28John_the_Evangelist_and_Peter%29_-_WGA7025.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer_-_The_Four_Holy_Men_%28John_the_Evangelist_and_Peter%29_-_WGA7025.jpg/260px-Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer_-_The_Four_Holy_Men_%28John_the_Evangelist_and_Peter%29_-_WGA7025.jpg 2x" data-file-width="560" data-file-height="1616" /></a><figcaption><i>John the Evangelist and Peter</i> by <a href="/wiki/Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer" title="Albrecht Dürer">Albrecht Dürer</a> (1526)</figcaption></figure>
<p>John is always mentioned in the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament#Lists_of_the_Twelve_Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">group of the first four apostles</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospels</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Acts" class="mw-redirect" title="Book of Acts">Book of Acts</a>, listed either second,<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> third<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> or fourth.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John, along with his brother James and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a>, formed an informal <a href="/wiki/Triumvirate" title="Triumvirate">triumvirate</a> among the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve Apostles">Twelve Apostles</a> in the Gospels. Jesus allowed them to be the only apostles present at three particular occasions during his public ministry, the <a href="/wiki/Raising_of_Jairus%27_daughter" title="Raising of Jairus&#39; daughter">Raising of Jairus' daughter</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus" title="Transfiguration of Jesus">Transfiguration of Jesus</a><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Agony_in_the_Garden" title="Agony in the Garden">Agony in the Garden of Gethsemane</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Jesus sent only Peter and John into the city to make the preparation for the final <a href="/wiki/Passover" title="Passover">Passover</a> meal (the <a href="/wiki/Last_Supper" title="Last Supper">Last Supper</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many traditions identify the "<a href="/wiki/Disciple_whom_Jesus_loved" title="Disciple whom Jesus loved">disciple whom Jesus loved</a>" in the Gospel of John as the Apostle John, but this identification is debated. At the meal itself, the "disciple whom Jesus loved" sat next to Jesus. It was customary to recline on couches at meals, and this disciple leaned on Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-butler_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-butler-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Tradition identifies this disciple as John.<sup id="cite_ref-bibleverse&#124;Jn&#124;13:23–25_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bibleverse|Jn|13:23–25-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>After the arrest of Jesus in the <a href="/wiki/Gethsemane" title="Gethsemane">Garden of Gethsemane</a>, only Peter and the "other disciple" (according to tradition, John) followed him into the palace of the <a href="/wiki/Caiaphas" title="Caiaphas">high-priest</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-butler_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-butler-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The "beloved disciple" alone, among the Apostles, remained near Jesus at the foot of the cross on <a href="/wiki/Calvary" title="Calvary">Calvary</a> alongside <a href="/wiki/Myrrhbearers" title="Myrrhbearers">myrrhbearers</a> and numerous other women. Following the instruction of Jesus from the Cross, the beloved disciple took <a href="/wiki/Mary,_the_mother_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary, the mother of Jesus">Mary, the mother of Jesus</a>, into his care as the last legacy of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Peter and John were also the only two apostles who ran to the empty tomb after <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a> bore witness to the <a href="/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus" title="Resurrection of Jesus">resurrection of Jesus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After Jesus' <a href="/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus_Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascension of Jesus Christ">Ascension</a> and the descent of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a> at <a href="/wiki/Pentecost" title="Pentecost">Pentecost</a>, John, together with Peter, took a prominent part in the founding and guidance of the church. He was with Peter at the healing of the lame man at <a href="/wiki/Solomon%27s_Porch" title="Solomon&#39;s Porch">Solomon's Porch</a> in the Temple<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and he was also thrown into prison with Peter.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Later, only Peter and John went to visit the newly converted believers in <a href="/wiki/Samaria" title="Samaria">Samaria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While he remained in Judea and the surrounding area, the other disciples returned to Jerusalem for the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic Council">Apostolic Council</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;48–50 AD</span>). Paul, in opposing his enemies in Galatia, recalls that John explicitly, along with Peter and <a href="/wiki/James_the_Just" class="mw-redirect" title="James the Just">James the Just</a>, were collectively recognized as the three <i><a href="/wiki/Early_Christianity#Jerusalem_church" title="Early Christianity">Pillars of the Church</a></i> and refers to the recognition that his Apostolic preaching of a <a href="/wiki/Abrogation_of_Old_Covenant_laws" title="Abrogation of Old Covenant laws">gospel free from Jewish Law</a> received from these three, the most prominent men of the messianic community at <a href="/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Jerusalem" class="mw-redirect" title="Early centers of Christianity">Jerusalem</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cathenc1_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cathenc1-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_disciple_whom_Jesus_loved">The disciple whom Jesus loved</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: The disciple whom Jesus loved">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Disciple_whom_Jesus_loved" title="Disciple whom Jesus loved">Disciple whom Jesus loved</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Johannesminne_BNM.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Johannesminne_BNM.jpg/170px-Johannesminne_BNM.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Johannesminne_BNM.jpg/255px-Johannesminne_BNM.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Johannesminne_BNM.jpg/340px-Johannesminne_BNM.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2017" data-file-height="2780" /></a><figcaption>Jesus and the Beloved Disciple</figcaption></figure>
<p>The phrase "the disciple whom Jesus loved as a brother" (<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">ὁ μαθητὴς ὃν ἠγάπα ὁ Ἰησοῦς</span></span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn">ho mathētēs hon ēgapā ho Iēsous</i></span>), or in <a href="/wiki/John_20:2" title="John 20:2">John 20:2</a>; "whom Jesus loved as a friend" (<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">ὃν ἐφίλει ὁ Ἰησοῦς</span></span>, <span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn">hon ephilei ho Iēsous</i></span>), is used six times in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> but in no other New Testament accounts of Jesus. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=John%2021:24&amp;version=nrsv">John 21:24</a> claims that the Gospel of John is based on the written testimony of this disciple.
</p><p>The disciple whom Jesus loved is referred to, specifically, six times in the Gospel of John:
</p>
<ul><li>It is this disciple who, while reclining beside Jesus at the <a href="/wiki/Last_Supper" title="Last Supper">Last Supper</a>, asks Jesus, after being requested by <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a> to do so, who it is that will betray him.<sup id="cite_ref-bibleverse&#124;Jn&#124;13:23–25_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bibleverse|Jn|13:23–25-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Later at the <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus" title="Crucifixion of Jesus">crucifixion</a>, Jesus tells his <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">mother</a>, "Woman, here is your son", and to the Beloved Disciple he says, "Here is your mother."<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>When <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a> discovers the empty tomb, she runs to tell the Beloved Disciple and Peter. The two men rush to the empty tomb and the Beloved Disciple is the first to reach the empty tomb. However, Peter is the first to enter.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In <a href="/wiki/John_21" title="John 21">John 21</a>, the last chapter of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a>, the Beloved Disciple is one of seven fishermen involved in the <a href="/wiki/Miraculous_catch_of_fish" title="Miraculous catch of fish">miraculous catch of 153 fish</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Also in the book's final chapter, after Jesus hints to Peter how Peter will die, Peter sees the Beloved Disciple following them and asks, "What about him?" Jesus answers, "If I want him to remain until I come, what is that to you? You follow Me!"<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Again in the Gospel's last chapter, it states that the very book itself is based on the written testimony of the disciple whom Jesus loved.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>None of the other Gospels has anyone in the parallel scenes that could be directly understood as the Beloved Disciple. For example, in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#24:12" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 24:12</a>, Peter alone runs to the tomb. Mark, Matthew and Luke do not mention any one of the twelve disciples having witnessed the crucifixion.
</p><p>There are also two references to an unnamed "other disciple" in <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+1:35–40&amp;version=nkjv">John 1:35–40</a> and <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+18:15–16&amp;version=nkjv">John 18:15–16</a>, which may be to the same person based on the wording in <a href="/wiki/John_20:2" title="John 20:2">John 20:2</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-brown_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brown-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="New_Testament_author">New Testament author</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: New Testament author">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pietro_Perugino_040.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Pietro_Perugino_040.jpg/260px-Pietro_Perugino_040.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="474" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Pietro_Perugino_040.jpg/390px-Pietro_Perugino_040.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Pietro_Perugino_040.jpg/520px-Pietro_Perugino_040.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2195" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>St. John at the <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus" title="Crucifixion of Jesus">Crucifixion of Jesus</a> in a <i><a href="/wiki/Stabat_Mater_(art)" title="Stabat Mater (art)">Stabat Mater</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Perugino" title="Pietro Perugino">Pietro Perugino</a>, c. 1482</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lamentation_of_the_Virgin_Rohan.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Lamentation_of_the_Virgin_Rohan.jpg/260px-Lamentation_of_the_Virgin_Rohan.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="369" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Lamentation_of_the_Virgin_Rohan.jpg/390px-Lamentation_of_the_Virgin_Rohan.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/86/Lamentation_of_the_Virgin_Rohan.jpg 2x" data-file-width="484" data-file-height="686" /></a><figcaption>Lamentation of the Virgin. John the Apostle trying to console <a href="/wiki/Mary,_mother_of_Jesus" title="Mary, mother of Jesus">Mary</a>, 1435</figcaption></figure>
<p>Church tradition has held that John is the author of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a> and four other books of the New Testament – the three <a href="/wiki/Epistles_of_John" class="mw-redirect" title="Epistles of John">Epistles of John</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a>. In the Gospel, authorship is internally credited to the "<a href="/wiki/Disciple_whom_Jesus_loved" title="Disciple whom Jesus loved">disciple whom Jesus loved</a>" (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">ὁ μαθητὴς ὃν ἠγάπα ὁ Ἰησοῦς</span></span>, <i>o mathētēs on ēgapa o Iēsous</i>) in <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=John%2020:2&amp;version=nrsv">John 20:2</a>. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=John%2021:24&amp;version=nrsv">John 21:24</a> claims that the Gospel of John is based on the written testimony of the "Beloved Disciple". The authorship of some <a href="/wiki/Johannine_literature" title="Johannine literature">Johannine literature</a> has been debated since about the year 200.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his 4th century <i><a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)">Ecclesiastical History</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> says that the <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_of_John" title="First Epistle of John">First Epistle of John</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a> are widely agreed upon as his. However, Eusebius mentions that the consensus is that the second and third epistles of John are not his but were written by some other John. Eusebius also goes to some length to establish with the reader that there is no general consensus regarding the revelation of John. The revelation of John could only be what is now called the Book of Revelation.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The Gospel according to John differs considerably from the <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels" title="Synoptic Gospels">Synoptic Gospels</a>, which were likely written decades earlier. The <a href="/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Anatolia" class="mw-redirect" title="Early centers of Christianity">bishops of Asia Minor</a> supposedly requested him to write his gospel to deal with the heresy of the <a href="/wiki/Ebionites" title="Ebionites">Ebionites</a>, who asserted that Christ did not exist before Mary. John probably knew of the Gospels of Matthew, Mark, and Luke, but these gospels spoke of Jesus primarily in the year following the imprisonment and death of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John the Baptist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Around 600, however, <a href="/wiki/Sophronius_of_Jerusalem" title="Sophronius of Jerusalem">Sophronius of Jerusalem</a> noted that "two epistles bearing his name ... are considered by some to be the work of a certain John the Elder" and, while stating that Revelation was written by John of Patmos, it was "later translated by Justin Martyr and Irenaeus,"<sup id="cite_ref-Theophylact_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Theophylact-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> presumably in an attempt to reconcile tradition with the obvious differences in Greek style.
</p><p>Until the 19th century, the authorship of the Gospel of John had been attributed to the Apostle John. However, most modern critical scholars have their doubts.<sup id="cite_ref-John_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Some scholars place the Gospel of John somewhere between AD 65 and 85;<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (March 2012)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> <a href="/wiki/John_Robinson_(bishop_of_Woolwich)" title="John Robinson (bishop of Woolwich)">John Robinson</a> proposes an initial edition by 50–55 and then a final edition by 65 due to narrative similarities with Paul.<sup id="cite_ref-Robinson_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Robinson-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: pp.284, 307">&#58;&#8202;pp.284,&#8202;307&#8202;</span></sup> Other scholars are of the opinion that the Gospel of John was composed in two or three stages.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: p.43">&#58;&#8202;p.43&#8202;</span></sup> Most contemporary scholars consider that the Gospel was not written until the latter third of the first century AD, and with the earliest possible date of AD 75–80: "...a date of AD 75–80 as the earliest possible date of composition for this Gospel."<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Other scholars think that an even later date, perhaps even the last decade of the first century AD right up to the start of the 2nd century (i.e. 90 – 100), is applicable.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nonetheless, today many theological scholars continue to accept the traditional authorship. Colin G. Kruse states that since John the Evangelist has been named consistently in the writings of early Church Fathers, "it is hard to pass by this conclusion, despite widespread reluctance to accept it by many, but by no means all, modern scholars."<sup id="cite_ref-Kruse_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kruse-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Modern, mainstream Bible scholars generally assert that the Gospel of John has been  written by an anonymous author.<sup id="cite_ref-sanders_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sanders-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ehrman_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ehrman-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lost_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lost-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Regarding whether the author of the Gospel of John was an eyewitness, according to Paul N. Anderson, the gospel "contains more direct claims to eyewitness origins than any of the other Gospel traditions."<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/F._F._Bruce" title="F. F. Bruce">F. F. Bruce</a> argues that 19:35 contains an "emphatic and explicit claim to eyewitness authority."<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> The gospel nowhere claims to have been written by direct witnesses to the reported events.<sup id="cite_ref-lost_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lost-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-iuda_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-iuda-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mainstream Bible scholars assert that all four gospels from the New Testament are fundamentally anonymous and most of mainstream scholars agree that these gospels have not been written by eyewitnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-Millard_2006_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Millard_2006-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201113,_42_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReddish201113,_42-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPerkinsCoogan20101380_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerkinsCoogan20101380-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECooganBrettlerNewsomPerkins20181380_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECooganBrettlerNewsomPerkins20181380-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> As <i>The New Oxford Annotated Bible</i> (2018) has put it, "Scholars generally agree that the Gospels were written forty to sixty years after the death of Jesus."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECooganBrettlerNewsomPerkins20181380_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECooganBrettlerNewsomPerkins20181380-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Book_of_Revelation">Book of Revelation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Book of Revelation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>According to the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a>, its author was on the <a href="/wiki/Patmos" title="Patmos">island of Patmos</a> "for the word of God and for the testimony of Jesus", when he was honoured with the <a href="/wiki/Vision_(religion)" class="mw-redirect" title="Vision (religion)">vision</a> contained in Revelation.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The author of the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a> identifies himself as "Ἰωάννης" ("John" in standard English translation).<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_Revelation,_Book_of_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_Revelation,_Book_of-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The early 2nd century writer, <a href="/wiki/Justin_Martyr" title="Justin Martyr">Justin Martyr</a>, was the first to equate the author of Revelation with <i>John the Apostle</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Justin_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Justin-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> However, most biblical scholars now contend that these were separate individuals since the text was written around 100 AD, after the death of John the Apostle,<sup id="cite_ref-John_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CHistoryIII39_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CHistoryIII39-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> although many historians have defended the identification of the Author of the Gospel of John with that of the Book of Revelation based on the similarity of the two texts.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/John_the_Presbyter" title="John the Presbyter">John the Presbyter</a>, an obscure figure in the early church, has also been identified with the seer of the Book of Revelation by such authors as <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> in his <i>Church History (Book III, 39)</i><sup id="cite_ref-CHistoryIII39_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CHistoryIII39-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Jerome" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Jerome">Jerome</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John is considered to have been exiled to <a href="/wiki/Patmos" title="Patmos">Patmos</a>, during the persecutions under Emperor <a href="/wiki/Domitian" title="Domitian">Domitian</a>. Revelation 1:9 says that the author wrote the book on Patmos: "I, John, both your brother and companion in tribulation, ... was on the island that is called Patmos for the word of God and for the testimony of Jesus Christ." <a href="/wiki/Adela_Yarbro_Collins" title="Adela Yarbro Collins">Adela Yarbro Collins</a>, a biblical scholar at <a href="/wiki/Yale_Divinity_School" title="Yale Divinity School">Yale Divinity School</a>, writes:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Early tradition says that John was banished to Patmos by the Roman authorities. This tradition is credible because banishment was a common punishment used during the Imperial period for a number of offenses. Among such offenses were the practices of magic and astrology. Prophecy was viewed by the Romans as belonging to the same category, whether Pagan, Jewish, or Christian. Prophecy with political implications, like that expressed by John in the book of Revelation, would have been perceived as a threat to Roman political power and order. Three of the islands in the <a href="/wiki/Sporades" title="Sporades">Sporades</a> were places where political offenders were banished. (Pliny <i>Natural History</i> 4.69–70; Tacitus <i>Annals</i> 4.30)<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Some modern critical scholars have raised the possibility that John the Apostle, John the Evangelist, and John of Patmos were three separate individuals.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> These scholars assert that John of Patmos wrote Revelation but neither the Gospel of John nor the Epistles of John. The author of Revelation identifies himself as "John" several times, but the author of the Gospel of John never identifies himself directly. Some Catholic scholars state that "vocabulary, grammar, and style make it doubtful that the book could have been put into its present form by the same person(s) responsible for the fourth gospel."<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Extrabiblical_traditions">Extrabiblical traditions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Extrabiblical traditions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Heilige_Johannes.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Heilige_Johannes.jpg/200px-Heilige_Johannes.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="260" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Heilige_Johannes.jpg/300px-Heilige_Johannes.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Heilige_Johannes.jpg/400px-Heilige_Johannes.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="3324" /></a><figcaption>Print of John the Apostle made at ca. the end of the 16th c. – the beginning of the 17th c.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Byzantinischer_Maler_um_1100_001.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Byzantinischer_Maler_um_1100_001.jpg/170px-Byzantinischer_Maler_um_1100_001.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Byzantinischer_Maler_um_1100_001.jpg/255px-Byzantinischer_Maler_um_1100_001.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Byzantinischer_Maler_um_1100_001.jpg/340px-Byzantinischer_Maler_um_1100_001.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1576" data-file-height="2189" /></a><figcaption>Byzantine <a href="/wiki/Illuminated_manuscript" title="Illuminated manuscript">illumination</a> depicting John dictating to his disciple, <a href="/wiki/Prochorus" class="mw-redirect" title="Prochorus">Prochorus</a> (<i>c.</i> 1100)</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tomb_of_Saint_John_the_Apostle.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Tomb_of_Saint_John_the_Apostle.jpg/220px-Tomb_of_Saint_John_the_Apostle.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Tomb_of_Saint_John_the_Apostle.jpg/330px-Tomb_of_Saint_John_the_Apostle.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Tomb_of_Saint_John_the_Apostle.jpg/440px-Tomb_of_Saint_John_the_Apostle.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>Tomb of Saint John the Apostle, <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._John" title="Basilica of St. John">Saint John's Basilica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sel%C3%A7uk" title="Selçuk">Selçuk</a>, Turkey</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Apocryphon_of_John" title="Apocryphon of John">Apocryphon of John</a></div>
<p>There is no information in the Bible concerning the duration of John's activity in <a href="/wiki/Iudaea_Province" class="mw-redirect" title="Iudaea Province">Judea</a>. According to tradition, John and the other Apostles remained some 12 years in this first field of labour. The <a href="/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians" title="Persecution of Christians">persecution of Christians</a> under <a href="/wiki/Herod_Agrippa_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Herod Agrippa I">Herod Agrippa I</a> (r. 41–44 AD) led to the scattering of the Apostles through the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Roman_provinces" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman provinces">provinces</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A messianic community existed at <a href="/wiki/Ephesus" title="Ephesus">Ephesus</a> before <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Paul's</a> first labors there (cf. "the brethren"),<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> in addition to <a href="/wiki/Priscilla_and_Aquila" title="Priscilla and Aquila">Priscilla and Aquila</a>. The original community was under the leadership of <a href="/wiki/Apollos" title="Apollos">Apollos</a> (1 Corinthians 1:12). They were disciples of John the Baptist and were converted by Aquila and Priscilla.<sup id="cite_ref-vailhe_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vailhe-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> According to tradition, after the <a href="/wiki/Assumption_of_Mary" title="Assumption of Mary">Assumption of Mary</a>, John went to Ephesus. <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> writes of "the church of Ephesus, founded by Paul, with John continuing with them until the times of <a href="/wiki/Trajan" title="Trajan">Trajan</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> From Ephesus he wrote the three epistles attributed to him. John was allegedly banished by the Roman authorities to the Greek island of <a href="/wiki/Patmos" title="Patmos">Patmos</a>, where, according to tradition, he wrote the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a>. According to <a href="/wiki/Tertullian" title="Tertullian">Tertullian</a> (in <i>The Prescription of Heretics</i>) John was banished (presumably to Patmos) after being plunged into boiling oil in Rome and suffering nothing from it. It is said that all in the audience of <a href="/wiki/Colosseum" title="Colosseum">Colosseum</a> were converted to Christianity upon witnessing this miracle. This event would have occurred in the late 1st century, during the reign of the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Domitian" title="Domitian">Domitian</a>, who was known for his persecution of Christians.
</p><p>When John was aged, he trained <a href="/wiki/Polycarp" title="Polycarp">Polycarp</a> who later became <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Smyrna" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Smyrna">Bishop of Smyrna</a>. This was important because Polycarp was able to carry John's message to future generations. Polycarp taught <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a>, passing on to him stories about John. Similarly, <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Ignatius of Antioch">Ignatius of Antioch</a> was a student of John. In <i><a href="/wiki/On_the_Detection_and_Overthrow_of_the_So-Called_Gnosis" class="mw-redirect" title="On the Detection and Overthrow of the So-Called Gnosis">Against Heresies</a></i>, Irenaeus relates how Polycarp told a story of 
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>John, the disciple of the Lord, going to bathe at Ephesus, and perceiving <a href="/wiki/Cerinthus" title="Cerinthus">Cerinthus</a> within, rushed out of the <a href="/wiki/Thermae" title="Thermae">bath-house</a> without bathing, exclaiming, "Let us fly, lest even the bath-house fall down, because <a href="/wiki/Cerinthus" title="Cerinthus">Cerinthus</a>, the enemy of the truth, is within."<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>It is traditionally believed that John was the youngest of the apostles and survived all of them. He is said to have lived to old age, dying of natural causes at Ephesus sometime after AD 98, during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Trajan" title="Trajan">Trajan</a>, thus becoming the only apostle who did not die as a <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyr" title="Christian martyr">martyr</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-zahn_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-zahn-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>An alternative account of John's death, ascribed by later Christian writers to the early second-century bishop <a href="/wiki/Papias_of_Hierapolis" title="Papias of Hierapolis">Papias of Hierapolis</a>, claims that he was slain by the Jews.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Most Johannine scholars doubt the reliability of its ascription to Papias, but a minority, including <a href="/wiki/B.W._Bacon" class="mw-redirect" title="B.W. Bacon">B.W. Bacon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Martin_Hengel" title="Martin Hengel">Martin Hengel</a> and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Barclay_Swete" title="Henry Barclay Swete">Henry Barclay Swete</a>, maintain that these references to Papias are credible.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Zahn argues that this reference is actually to <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John the Baptist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-zahn_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-zahn-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> John's traditional tomb is thought to be located in the former <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._John" title="Basilica of St. John">basilica of Saint John</a> at <a href="/wiki/Sel%C3%A7uk" title="Selçuk">Selçuk</a>, a small town in the vicinity of Ephesus.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John is also associated with the <a href="/wiki/Pseudepigrapha" title="Pseudepigrapha">pseudepigraphal</a> <a href="/wiki/New_Testament_apocrypha" title="New Testament apocrypha">apocryphal</a> text of <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_John" title="Acts of John">the Acts of John</a>, which is traditionally viewed as written by John himself or his disciple, <a href="/wiki/Leucius_Charinus" title="Leucius Charinus">Leucius Charinus</a>. It was widely circulated by the second century CE but deemed heretical at the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Nicaea" title="Second Council of Nicaea">Second Council of Nicaea</a> (787 CE). Varying fragments survived in Greek and Latin within monastic libraries. It contains strong <a href="/wiki/Docetism" title="Docetism">docetic</a> themes, but is not considered in modern scholarship to be <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnostic.</a><sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Liturgical_commemoration">Liturgical commemoration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Liturgical commemoration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> of Saint John in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>, which calls him "Saint John, Apostle and Evangelist", and in the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a> and Lutheran Calendars, which call him "Saint John the Apostle and Evangelist", is on 27 December.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Tridentine Calendar">Tridentine Calendar</a> he was commemorated also on each of the following days up to and including 3 January, the <a href="/wiki/Octave_(liturgy)" title="Octave (liturgy)">Octave</a> of the 27 December feast. This Octave was abolished by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> in 1955.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The traditional liturgical color is white. John, <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">Apostle</a> and <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">Evangelist</a> is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Festival (Anglicanism)">Festival</a> on 27 December.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> In Roman Catholic tradition he considered patron of <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> and <a href="/wiki/Turkish_people" title="Turkish people">Turkish people</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-M._Limberis_2011_164_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-M._Limberis_2011_164-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Until 1960, another feast day which appeared in the General Roman Calendar is that of "Saint John Before the Latin Gate" on 6 May, celebrating a tradition recounted by Jerome that St John was brought to Rome during the reign of the Emperor Domitian, and was thrown in a vat of boiling oil, from which he was miraculously preserved unharmed. A church (<a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_a_Porta_Latina" title="San Giovanni a Porta Latina">San Giovanni a Porta Latina</a>) dedicated to him was built near the <a href="/wiki/Porta_Latina" title="Porta Latina">Latin gate</a> of Rome, the traditional site of this event.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> and those <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a> which follow the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a> commemorate the "Repose of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist John the Theologian" on 26 September.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> On 8 May they celebrate the "<a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">Feast</a> of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist John the Theologian", on which date Christians used to draw forth from his grave fine ashes which were believed to be effective for healing the sick.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> John is also commemorated on 30 June as a member of the twelve apostles.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> There is also a commemoration of the <a href="/wiki/Synaxis" title="Synaxis">synaxis</a> of the Apostle John at Diaconissa on 15 February.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_views">Other views</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Other views">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Islamic_view">Islamic view</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Islamic view">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a> also speaks of Jesus's disciples but does not mention their names, instead referring to them as "supporters for [the cause of] Allah".<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The Sunnah did not mention their names either. However, some Muslim scholars mentioned their names,<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> likely relying on the resources of Christians, who are considered "<a href="/wiki/People_of_the_Book" title="People of the Book">People of the Book</a>" in Islamic tradition. Muslim <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a> more or less agrees with the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> list and says that the disciples included <a href="/wiki/Peter_in_Islam" title="Peter in Islam">Peter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philip_the_Apostle" title="Philip the Apostle">Philip</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle" title="Thomas the Apostle">Thomas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bartholomew_the_Apostle" title="Bartholomew the Apostle">Bartholomew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Matthew_the_Apostle" title="Matthew the Apostle">Matthew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew</a>, <a href="/wiki/James,_son_of_Zebedee" class="mw-redirect" title="James, son of Zebedee">James</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jude_the_Apostle" title="Jude the Apostle">Jude</a>, John and <a href="/wiki/Simon_the_Zealot" title="Simon the Zealot">Simon the Zealot</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Notably, narrations of People of the Book (Christians and Jews) are not to be believed or disbelieved by Muslims as long as there is nothing that supports or denies them in Quran or Sunnah.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Latter-day_Saint_view">Latter-day Saint view</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Latter-day Saint view">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Account_of_John" title="Account of John">Account of John</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> (LDS Church) teaches that, "John is mentioned frequently in latter-day revelation (1 Ne. 14:18–27; 3 Ne. 28:6; Ether 4:16; D&amp;C 7; 27:12; 61:14; 77; 88:141). For Latter-day Saints these passages confirm the biblical record of John and also provide insight into his greatness and the importance of the work the Lord has given him to do on the earth in New Testament times and in the last days. The latter-day scriptures clarify that John did not die but was allowed to remain on the earth as a ministering servant until the time of the Lord's Second Coming (John 21:20–23; 3 Ne. 28:6–7; D&amp;C 7)".<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> It also teaches that in 1829, along with the resurrected Peter and the resurrected James, John visited <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Smith" title="Joseph Smith">Joseph Smith</a> and <a href="/wiki/Oliver_Cowdery" title="Oliver Cowdery">Oliver Cowdery</a> and restored the <a href="/wiki/Priesthood_(Latter_Day_Saints)" title="Priesthood (Latter Day Saints)">priesthood</a> authority with <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_succession" title="Apostolic succession">Apostolic succession</a> to earth,<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> though some ex-Latter-Day Saints claim that previous editions of latter-day scripture contradict this claim of priesthood authority and Apostolic Succession.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> John, along with the <a href="/wiki/Three_Nephites" title="Three Nephites">Three Nephites</a>, will live to see the <a href="/wiki/Second_Coming_of_Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Coming of Christ">Second Coming of Christ</a> as <a href="/wiki/Translation_(Mormonism)" title="Translation (Mormonism)">translated beings</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ldsbd_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ldsbd-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-Day Saints teaches that John the Apostle is the same person as John the Evangelist, John of Patmos, and the Beloved Disciple.<sup id="cite_ref-ldsbd_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ldsbd-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_art">In art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: In art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Helsinki_Cathedral_John_the_Evangelist.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Helsinki_Cathedral_John_the_Evangelist.jpg/170px-Helsinki_Cathedral_John_the_Evangelist.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Helsinki_Cathedral_John_the_Evangelist.jpg/255px-Helsinki_Cathedral_John_the_Evangelist.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Helsinki_Cathedral_John_the_Evangelist.jpg/340px-Helsinki_Cathedral_John_the_Evangelist.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3008" data-file-height="3888" /></a><figcaption>Statue of John the Evangelist by August Wredow on <a href="/wiki/Helsinki_Cathedral" title="Helsinki Cathedral">Helsinki Cathedral</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St_John_the_Apostle_by_Jacques_Bellange.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/St_John_the_Apostle_by_Jacques_Bellange.jpg/170px-St_John_the_Apostle_by_Jacques_Bellange.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="290" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/St_John_the_Apostle_by_Jacques_Bellange.jpg/255px-St_John_the_Apostle_by_Jacques_Bellange.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/St_John_the_Apostle_by_Jacques_Bellange.jpg/340px-St_John_the_Apostle_by_Jacques_Bellange.jpg 2x" data-file-width="879" data-file-height="1499" /></a><figcaption>St. John the Apostle by <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Bellange" title="Jacques Bellange">Jacques Bellange</a>, c. 1600</figcaption></figure>
<p>As he was traditionally identified with the beloved apostle, the evangelist, and the author of the Revelation and several Epistles, John played an extremely prominent role in art from the early Christian period onward.<sup id="cite_ref-Hall1979_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall1979-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> He is traditionally depicted in one of two distinct ways: either as an aged man with a white or gray beard, or alternatively as a beardless youth.<sup id="cite_ref-HallJerousek_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HallJerousek-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BritannicaOnline_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritannicaOnline-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> The first way of depicting him was more common in <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_art" title="Byzantine art">Byzantine art</a>, where it was possibly influenced by antique depictions of <a href="/wiki/Socrates" title="Socrates">Socrates</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> the second was more common in the art of <a href="/wiki/Medieval_art" title="Medieval art">Medieval Western Europe</a>, and can be dated back as far as 4th century Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-BritannicaOnline_116-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritannicaOnline-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Legends from the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_John" title="Acts of John">Acts of John</a>, an apocryphal text attributed to John, contributed much to Medieval iconography; it is the source of the idea that John became an apostle at a young age.<sup id="cite_ref-BritannicaOnline_116-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritannicaOnline-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> One of John's familiar attributes is the chalice, often with a serpent emerging from it.<sup id="cite_ref-Hall1979_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall1979-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> This symbol is interpreted as a reference to a legend from the Acts of John,<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> in which John was challenged to drink a cup of poison to demonstrate the power of his faith (the poison being symbolized by the serpent).<sup id="cite_ref-Hall1979_114-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall1979-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Other common attributes include a book or scroll, in reference to the writings traditionally attributed to him, and an eagle,<sup id="cite_ref-BritannicaOnline_116-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritannicaOnline-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> which is argued to symbolize the high-soaring, inspirational quality of these writings.<sup id="cite_ref-Hall1979_114-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall1979-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Medieval and through to Renaissance works of painting, sculpture and literature, Saint John is often presented in an androgynous or feminized manner.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Historians have related such portrayals to the circumstances of the believers for whom they were intended.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> For instance, John's feminine features are argued to have helped to make him more relatable to women.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Likewise, Sarah McNamer argues that because of his status as an androgynous saint, John could function as an "image of a <a href="/wiki/Third_gender" title="Third gender">third or mixed gender</a>"<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> and "a crucial figure with whom to identify"<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> for male believers who sought to cultivate an attitude of <a href="/wiki/Affective_piety" title="Affective piety">affective piety</a>, a highly emotional style of devotion that, in late-medieval culture, was thought to be poorly compatible with masculinity.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> After the Middle Ages, feminizing portrayals of Saint John continued to be made; a case in point is an etching by <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Bellange" title="Jacques Bellange">Jacques Bellange</a>, shown to the right, described by art critic <a href="/wiki/Richard_Dorment" title="Richard Dorment">Richard Dorment</a> as depicting "a softly androgynous creature with a corona of frizzy hair, small breasts like a teenage girl, and the round belly of a mature woman."<sup id="cite_ref-dorment_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dorment-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the realm of popular media, this latter phenomenon was brought to notice in <a href="/wiki/Dan_Brown" title="Dan Brown">Dan Brown</a>'s novel <i><a href="/wiki/The_Da_Vinci_Code" title="The Da Vinci Code">The Da Vinci Code</a></i> (2003), where one of the book's characters suggests that the feminine-looking person to Jesus' right in <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Supper_(Leonardo)" title="The Last Supper (Leonardo)">The Last Supper</a></i> is actually <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a> rather than St. John.
</p>

			<div class="thumb" style="width: 205px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:An%C3%B4nimo_-_A_%C3%9Altima_Ceia.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="The Last Supper, anonymous painter"><img alt="The Last Supper, anonymous painter" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/An%C3%B4nimo_-_A_%C3%9Altima_Ceia.jpg/155px-An%C3%B4nimo_-_A_%C3%9Altima_Ceia.jpg" decoding="async" width="155" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/An%C3%B4nimo_-_A_%C3%9Altima_Ceia.jpg/233px-An%C3%B4nimo_-_A_%C3%9Altima_Ceia.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/An%C3%B4nimo_-_A_%C3%9Altima_Ceia.jpg/311px-An%C3%B4nimo_-_A_%C3%9Altima_Ceia.jpg 2x" data-file-width="618" data-file-height="795" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
<p><i>The Last Supper</i>, anonymous painter
</p>
			</div>
		</li>
</ul>

<li id="cite_note-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-3">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Nor do we have reliable accounts from later times. What we have are legends, about some of the apostles – chiefly Peter, Paul, Thomas, Andrew, and John. But the apocryphal Acts that tell their stories are indeed highly apocryphal.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Bart D. Ehrman, "Were the Disciples Martyred for Believing the Resurrection? A Blast From the Past", ehrmanblog.org</cite></div></blockquote> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>"The big problem with this argument [of who would die for a lie] is that it assumes precisely what we don’t know. We don't know how most of the disciples died. The next time someone tells you they were all martyred, ask them how they know. Or better yet, ask them which ancient source they are referring to that says so. The reality is [that] we simply do not have reliable information about what happened to Jesus' disciples after he died. In fact, we scarcely have any information about them while they were still living, nor do we have reliable accounts from later times. What we have are legends."</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Bart Ehrman, Emerson Green, "Who Would Die for a Lie?"</cite></div></blockquote></span>

<li id="cite_note-91"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-91">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFCheyne1901" class="citation encyclopaedia cs1">Cheyne, Thomas Kelly (1901). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/encyclopaediabib02cheyuoft/page/n711/mode/1up">"John, Son of Zebedee"</a>. <i><a href="/wiki/Encyclopaedia_Biblica" title="Encyclopaedia Biblica">Encyclopaedia Biblica</a></i>. Vol.&#160;2. Adam &amp; Charles Black. pp.&#160;2509–11.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=John%2C+Son+of+Zebedee&amp;rft.btitle=Encyclopaedia+Biblica&amp;rft.pages=2509-11&amp;rft.pub=Adam+%26+Charles+Black&amp;rft.date=1901&amp;rft.aulast=Cheyne&amp;rft.aufirst=Thomas+Kelly&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Farchive.org%2Fdetails%2Fencyclopaediabib02cheyuoft%2Fpage%2Fn711%2Fmode%2F1up&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJohn+the+Apostle" class="Z3988"></span> Although Papias' works are no longer extant, the fifth-century ecclesiastical historian <a href="/wiki/Philip_of_Side" title="Philip of Side">Philip of Side</a> and the ninth-century monk <a href="/wiki/George_Hamartolos" title="George Hamartolos">George Hamartolos</a> both stated that Papias had written that John was "slain by the Jews."</span>

<li id="cite_note-92"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-92">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFRasimus2010" class="citation book cs1">Rasimus, Tuomas (2010). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=pN07vMoBsokC&amp;pg=PA5"><i>The Legacy of John: Second-Century Reception of the Fourth Gospel</i></a>. Brill. p.&#160;5. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-9-00417633-1" title="Special:BookSources/978-9-00417633-1"><bdi>978-9-00417633-1</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Legacy+of+John%3A+Second-Century+Reception+of+the+Fourth+Gospel&amp;rft.pages=5&amp;rft.pub=Brill&amp;rft.date=2010&amp;rft.isbn=978-9-00417633-1&amp;rft.aulast=Rasimus&amp;rft.aufirst=Tuomas&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DpN07vMoBsokC%26pg%3DPA5&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJohn+the+Apostle" class="Z3988"></span>  Rasimus finds corroborating evidence for this tradition in "two martyrologies from Edessa and Carthage" and writes that "Mark 10:35–40//Matt. 20:20–23 can be taken to portray Jesus predicting the martyrdom of both the sons of Zebedee."</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-117"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-117">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">
Jadranka Prolović, "Socrates and St. John the Apostle: the interchangеable similarity of their portraits" <i>Zograf,</i> vol. 35 (2011), 9: "It is difficult to locate when and where this iconography of John originated and what the prototype was, yet it is clearly visible that this iconography of John contains all of the main characteristics of well-known antique images of Socrates. This fact leads to the conclusion that Byzantine artists used depictions of Socrates as a model for the portrait of John."</span>
